{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Diamond A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}b}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}

\scriptstyle R\mathsf{rf}+\mathord{\Box}^{\bullet}+\and^{\bullet}\;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}

\scriptstyle\mathbin{\supset}^{\circ}\;

\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}

Fig. 4:
Left: Proof in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}b}\}
and

\mathfrak{G}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}
.
Right: Diagrammatic representation of the top left sequent in the derivation.

4
Preliminaries: Fully Labelled Sequents

In this section we present the fully labelled sequent proof system
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}

[
20
]
for intuitionistic modal logics we are using in order to prove decidability of
\mathsf{IS4}
. The starting point is the notion of a
labelled formula
which is a pair
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
of a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
.
A
relational atom
is either an expression
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
are labels.
A
(labelled) sequent

\mathfrak{G}
is a triple
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta

where
\mathcal{R}
is a set of relational atoms and
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are multisets of labelled formulas, all written as comma-separated lists.
Figure
2
shows the inference rules of
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
.
1
1

1

Note that
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
has no cut rule, meaning that cut is admissible. For a syntactic proof of cut elimination, see
[
20
]
.

Figure
3
shows some additional rules, and we define
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}\cup\{\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet},\mathsf{weak},\mathsf{4}^{\bullet},\mathsf{4}^{\circ},\mathsf{cont}^{\bullet},\mathsf{cont}^{\circ}\}
.

A
derivation
is a finite tree where each edge is a sequent and each internal node is a rule.
A derivation is a
proof
of the sequent at the root iff each leaf is a rule with no premises, i.e.,
\mathord{\bot}^{\bullet}
or
\mathsf{id}
.
As shown in
[
20
]
,
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
is sound and complete for
\mathsf{IS4}
.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
20
]
)
.

A formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
is a theorem of
\mathsf{IS4}
iff for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, the sequent
\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
is derivable in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
.

Example 4.2
.

On the left of Fig.
4
we have a proof in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
for the valid formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, where, for reasons of space, several rules are sometimes condensed into a single step.

Corollary 4.3
.

Rules
\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet}
,
\mathsf{weak}
,
\mathsf{4}^{\bullet}
,
\mathsf{4}^{\circ}
,
\mathsf{cont}^{\bullet}
, and
\mathsf{cont}^{\circ}
are admissible for
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
. Therefore, a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
is derivable in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
iff it is derivable in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
.

5
From Sequents to Models

In this section we establish the promised correspondence between sequents and models.

Notation 5.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a sequent
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta
. We write

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
occurs in
\mathcal{R}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
occurs in
\mathcal{R}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
occurs in
\Gamma

(in this case we also say that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}

occurs
at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in
\mathfrak{G}
);

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
occurs in
\Delta

(in this case we also say that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}

occurs
at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in
\mathfrak{G}
);

•

\ell(\mathfrak{G})
for the set of labels occurring in
\mathfrak{G}
.

This notation is used extensively throughout the paper.
2
2

2

The use of
\bullet
and
\circ
in this way goes back to Lamarche
[
18
]
.

Example 5.2
.

This notation also enables us to represent sequents graphically, as shown on the right of Fig.
4
, which
depicts the top-left sequent of the proof showcased on the left of the figure. The sequent is represented by means of a directed graph whose nodes are the labels of the sequent (for convenience we shall use natural numbers), dashed edges are the
\leq
-relations, and solid edges are the
R
-relations. We can now simply write a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
) next to a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}
, to indicate that the labelled formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
occurs on the left-hand side (right-hand side) of
\Longrightarrow
.

Definition 5.3

(Happy labelled formula)
.

A formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
) is
happy
at a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
(
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
) is
happy
for short, iff the following conditions hold:

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
is always happy;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\circ}
is happy iff we do not have
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\bullet}
is never happy;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\circ}
is always happy;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\and B}^{\bullet}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\and B}^{\circ}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\vee}B}^{\bullet}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\vee}B}^{\circ}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}^{\bullet}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}^{\circ}
is happy iff

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}^{\bullet}
is happy iff

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}^{\bullet}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}^{\circ}
is happy iff

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}^{\bullet}
is happy iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
;

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}^{\circ}
is happy iff

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}^{\circ}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.

Otherwise, the formula is
unhappy
.

Definition 5.4

(Happy label)
.

A label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
occurring in a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
happy
iff all formulas occurring at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in
\mathfrak{G}
are happy. Label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is
almost happy
iff all formulas occurring at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
are happy except, possibly, those of the shapes
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
.
Label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is
naively happy
iff all formulas occurring at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
are happy except, possibly, those
of the shapes
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
.

Definition 5.5

(Structurally saturated sequent)
.

A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
structurally saturated
iff the following holds:

(
\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet}
)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
, then
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
;

(
\mathsf{F_{1}}
)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
, then there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
;

(
\mathsf{F_{2}}
)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
, then there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
;

(
\mathord{\leq}\mathsf{tr}
)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
;

(
\mathord{\leq}\mathsf{rf}
)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
occurring in
\mathfrak{G}
;

(
R\mathsf{tr}
)

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
;

(
R\mathsf{rf}
)

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
occurring in
\mathfrak{G}
.

Definition 5.6

(Happy sequent)
.

A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
happy
iff it is structurally saturated and all labels in the sequent are happy.

Definition 5.7

(Model of a sequent)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a sequent. We define the
model
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}}
of

\mathfrak{G}
to be the quadruple

\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}}=\langle\ell(\mathfrak{G}),\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}},\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}},V\rangle
where
\ell(\mathfrak{G}),\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}},\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}
are as defined in Notation
5.1
and
V
is the
function
V\colon\ell(\mathfrak{G})\to 2^{\mathcal{A}}
defined such that for all atoms
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}\in\mathcal{A}
we have

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}\in V({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w})
iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!w}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
.

Theorem 5.8

(restate = completeness, name = Completeness)
.

For a happy sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, its model
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}}=\langle\ell(\mathfrak{G}),\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}},\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}},V\rangle
is a transitive and reflexive birelational model with the following two properties:

•

if
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
;

•

if
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
, then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
.

Definition 5.9

(Axiomatic sequent)
.

A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
axiomatic
iff there is a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
such that either
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\circ}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}
, or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\bullet}
. Otherwise,
\mathfrak{G}
is called
non-axiomatic
.

Remark 5.10
.

An axiomatic sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is never happy, because either
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\circ}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\bullet}
is unhappy.

6
Layers, Clusters, and Saturated Sequents

All derivations of
\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
obtained by a proof search in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
have a particular structure: their labels are partitioned into
layers
, with each layer having a tree structure. This plays an important role in our proof search algorithm.

Definition 6.1

(Layer)
.

For a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, we define the relation
R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}
to be the transitive and reflexive closure of
R_{\mathfrak{G}}\cup R_{\mathfrak{G}}^{-1}
. Since this is an equivalence relation, we can define
a
layer

L
in
\mathfrak{G}
to be an equivalence class of
R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}
.

Definition 6.2

(Layered sequent)
.

We say that a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
layered
iff for any labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}
occurring in
\mathfrak{G}
:

1.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\not\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\not\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
; and

2.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{\not\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
.

For layers
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, we define
L_{1}\leq L_{2}
whenever there are labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in L_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in L_{2}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
. We write
L_{1}<L_{2}
iff
L_{1}\leq L_{2}
and
L_{1}\neq L_{2}
.

Proposition 6.3

(restate = OrderLayers, name = )
.

For a layered structurally saturated sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, relation
\leq
is an order relation on its layers.

The second condition in Def.
6.2
is only needed to establish antisymmetry in Prop.
6.3
. If
\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}
on labels is already antisymmetric then the second condition follows from the first. In all sequents that we discuss here, the order relation
\leq
on layers defines a tree structure.

Definition 6.4

(Tree-layered sequent)
.

A layered sequent is
tree-layered
iff (i) there is a layer
L_{0}
such that
L_{0}\leq L
for all layers
L
, and (ii) for all layers
L
,
L^{\prime}
, and
L^{\prime\prime}
, whenever
L^{\prime}\leq L
and
L^{\prime\prime}\leq L
, then either
L^{\prime}\leq L^{\prime\prime}
or
L^{\prime\prime}\leq L^{\prime}
.

Definition 6.5

(Topmost and inner layer)
.

A layer
L
in a layered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is called a
topmost layer
iff it is maximal with respect to
\leq
, i.e., whenever
L\leq L^{\prime}
for some layer
L^{\prime}
, then
L=L^{\prime}
. Otherwise, the layer
L
is called an
inner layer
.

Note that in a tree-layered structurally saturated sequent, topmost layers are the leaves of the tree structure w.r.t.
\leq
.

In a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
constructed by
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
, each layer also has a tree structure with respect to
R_{\mathfrak{G}}
. But, as said before, in order to search for a proof and a countermodel at the same time, we need to weaken this tree structure on the layers.

Definition 6.6

(Cluster)
.

If
\mathfrak{G}
is structurally saturated, then
R_{\mathfrak{G}}\cap R_{\mathfrak{G}}^{-1}
is an equivalence relation, and we can define a
cluster

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
in
\mathfrak{G}
to be an equivalence class
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}},...,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}\}
of
R_{\mathfrak{G}}\cap R_{\mathfrak{G}}^{-1}
. A cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}}\}
containing only one label is called
singleton
. On clusters, we define two binary relations:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}}
iff for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
iff there are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}_{2}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.

We sometimes abuse the notation and replace one of the clusters by a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
even when
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\}
is not a cluster. Nevertheless, the definitions are then applied verbatim to
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\}
.

Proposition 6.7

(restate = OrderClusters, name = )
.

For a structurally saturated sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, relation
R_{\mathfrak{G}}
is an order and
\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}
is a preorder on its clusters. If
\mathfrak{G}
is layered, then
\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}
is also an order.

Definition 6.8

(Tree-clustered sequent)
.

A structurally saturated sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is called
tree-clustered
iff (i) for any clusters
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}
there is a cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}
, and (ii) for all clusters
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}
, whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
, then either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C^{\prime}}
.

Definition 6.9

(Happy layer)
.

A layer
L
is
happy/almost happy/naively happy
iff all labels of
L
are happy/almost happy/naively happy respectively.

Remark 6.10
.

Observe that the definitions of happy labelled formulas for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
in Def.
5.3
are different from the forcing conditions of the corresponding connectives.
This mismatch is intentional with the missing conditions on
\leq
outsourced to
(\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet})
-structural saturation (Def.
5.5
) instead. As a result, happiness becomes almost a local property for layers: if a sequent is modified (by adding labelled formulas or relational atoms) outside of a given happy layer, the only formulas whose happiness needs to be reinspected are
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
. We use this observation in the notion of stable sequents below, where inner layers are never modified and always remain happy.

Definition 6.11

(Stable and saturated sequent)
.

A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
stable
iff it is tree-layered and tree-clustered and, additionally, all its inner layers are happy. A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
saturated
iff it is stable and all its topmost layers are almost happy. It is
semi-saturated
iff it is stable and all its topmost layers are naively happy. A set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
of sequents is called
stable/saturated/semi-saturated
iff it is finite and all elements of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
are stable/saturated/semi-saturated respectively.

Since tree-layered sequents are layered and tree-clustered sequents are structurally saturated (in fact, the definition of clusters does not make sense otherwise), all stable sequents are layered and structurally saturated.

7
Search Algorithm

The algorithm that we will present here works on stable sequents. Roughly speaking, we work on the topmost layers of such sequents until these layers are almost happy. This is achieved by first semi-saturating (which essentially means applying all inference rules to a layer that do not add new labels to the sequent), then picking an unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
in the layer and making it happy (either by applying the
\mathord{\Diamond}^{\bullet}
-rule or, in case of loop detection, by creating a cluster). Then we semi-saturate again, and continue until all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
in the layer are happy and, therefore, the layer is almost happy.

After all layers are almost happy, the only unhappy formulas remaining in the resulting saturated sequent are in topmost layers and of the shape
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
. For the first two shapes, the sequent is axiomatic, and we can stop working on it.

If all unhappy formulas are in topmost layers and of the shape
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, we make them happy by creating new layers that become new topmost layers to be saturated while formerly topmost layers turn into happy inner layers. This process is repeated, until we see a repetition of layers. In order to achieve termination of this process, we implement two other loop detection mechanisms (different from the one used for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
formulas).

In the remainder of this section, we formally introduce the concepts needed to understand the details of this algorithm, which is then shown in Fig.
7
.

7-A

Semi-saturation

We begin by making happy all formulas that are not of the form
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
.

Definition 7.1

(Semi-saturation)
.

We define a rewrite relation
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
on sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
of sequents:
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
iff there is a sequent
\mathfrak{G}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
such that some labelled formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
of one of the below shapes occurs at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in
\mathfrak{G}
and is unhappy, and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is obtained by replacing sequent
\mathfrak{G}
in set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
either with sequent
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
according to cases 1)–4) below, or with sequents
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{G^{\prime\prime}}
according to cases 5)–7) below,
as follows:

1.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\and B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
all of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
that are missing;

2.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\vee}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
all of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
that are missing;

3.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
that are missing whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
;

4.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
that are missing whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
;

5.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\vee}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
(respectively
\mathfrak{G^{\prime\prime}}
) by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
the labelled formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
(respectively
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
);

6.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\and B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
(respectively
\mathfrak{G^{\prime\prime}}
) by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
the labelled formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
(respectively
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
);

7.

for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, obtain
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
(respectively
\mathfrak{G^{\prime\prime}}
) by adding to
\mathfrak{G}
the labelled formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
(respectively
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
).

We write
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\ast}
for the transitive and reflexive closure of
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
. If
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\ast}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is in normal form w.r.t.
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
, i.e., if all labels occurring in all sequents from
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
are naively happy, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is called the
semi-saturation
of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.

The use of this term is justified because, the semi-saturation of a stable set of sequents is semi-saturated.

Lemma 7.2

(restate = SaturationStable, name = )
.

All the following statements hold:

1.

If a set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is stable and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is stable.

2.

On finite sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
, the rewrite relation
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
is terminating.

3.

A set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is semi-saturated iff it is stable and in normal form w.r.t.
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
.

7-B

Saturation

In the next step we show how to deal with unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
. This is the first source of non-termination, and for this we employ the same method that is commonly used for classical
\mathsf{S4}
. However, whereas in the case of classical
\mathsf{S4}
, loop detection completes the proof search in the current branch, here we have to continue proof search. For this reason we realize loops by clusters.

Definition 7.3

(Equivalent labels/clusters)
.

Labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
occurring in sequents
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{H}
respectively are
equivalent
, in symbols
{{}_{\mathfrak{G}\mathstrut^{\mathstrut}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\sim{{}_{\mathfrak{H}\mathstrut^{\mathstrut}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
, iff
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}\Longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{H},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\bullet}
and also
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}\Longleftrightarrow\mathfrak{H},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}^{\circ}
for all formulas
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
.
If
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{H}
are clear from the context, we simply write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.
For structurally saturated sequents
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{H}
, we can generalize this to clusters:
{{}_{\mathfrak{G}\mathstrut^{\mathstrut}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\sim{{}_{\mathfrak{H}\mathstrut^{\mathstrut}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}}
iff there is a bijection
f\colon{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}fx}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}
.

Definition 7.4

(Having no past)
.

A label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in a sequent
\mathfrak{G}

has no past
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.

Definition 7.5

(Saturation)
.

We define a binary relation
\mathbin{\rightsquigarrow_{\Diamond}}
on sequents as follows:
\mathfrak{G}\mathbin{\rightsquigarrow_{\Diamond}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
iff there is a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
,
such that
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}^{\bullet}
is unhappy, and all labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
but not
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
are almost happy, and either

1.

Option 1:

there is
a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}^{\bullet}
is happy, and every label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
has no past in
\mathfrak{G}
. Then
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{G}
by first substituting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for each occurrence of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and then closing the resulting
R_{\mathfrak{G}}
under transitivity;
or

2.

Option 2: There is no such label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
. Then
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{G}
by first adding
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
for some fresh label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
, and then closing the resulting
R_{\mathfrak{G}}
under transitivity and reflexivity.

For sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
of sequents, we write
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}

iff
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is semi-saturated,
\mathfrak{G}\mathbin{\rightsquigarrow_{\Diamond}}\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{G}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is a semi-saturation of
\bigl(\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\setminus\{\mathfrak{G}\}\bigr)\cup\{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\}
.
3
3

3

Strictly speaking, in the first case
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is already semi-saturated, but to simplify the presentation, we semi-saturate in both cases.

We write
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}^{\ast}_{\Diamond}}
for the transitive and reflexive closure of
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
.
If
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}^{\ast}_{\Diamond}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is in normal form w.r.t.
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is called a
saturation
of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.

As before, this term is justified because, a saturation of a semi-saturated set of sequents is saturated.

Lemma 7.6

(restate = DiaSaturationStable, name = )
.

All the following statements hold:

1.

If a set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is
semi-saturated
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is
semi-saturated.

2.

The rewrite relation
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
is terminating.

3.

A set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is saturated iff it is semi-saturated and in normal form w.r.t.
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
.

7-C

Lifting saturation

After having discussed how to saturate sets of sequents, we are now going to show how to deal with unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}^{\circ}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}^{\circ}
. For this, we have to create new layers. The naive way would be to just copy the
R
-structure of the layer that contains the unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
. However, due to the presence of clusters, we sometimes need to create two copies of the cluster that contains the unhappy formula.

Definition 7.7

(Sequent sum)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
be sequents
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
respectively. We define their
sum

{\mathfrak{G}}+{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}}
to be the sequent
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
.

Construction 7.8

(Layer Lifting I)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a saturated sequent,
L
be one of its topmost layers, and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in L
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
belongs to a cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{x}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{h}}\}
with
h\geq 1
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{m}}
for some
1\leq m\leq h
, which we often abbreviate as
m=1..h
. In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i^{\prime}}}
for any
i,i^{\prime}=1..h
.
Let
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{l}}\}=L\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{x}}
where
l\geq 0
.
Then, if
h=1
, let
{\hat{L}}
be the set of fresh labels
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{l}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}\}
. Otherwise, if
h>1
, let

{\hat{L}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{l}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{1}^{\prime}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{h}^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{1}^{\prime\prime}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{h}^{\prime\prime}}\}

where again all labels are fresh. We define
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
to consist of:

1.

relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\in{\hat{L}}
;

2.

for each
i=1..l
, each
j=1..h
, and each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\in\ell(\mathfrak{G})
:

(a)

relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}}
,

(b)

relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
,

(c)

only for
h>1
: relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{j}^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{j}^{\prime\prime}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}w}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{j}}}
;

3.

for all
i,i^{\prime}=1..l
and
j,j^{\prime}=1..h
,

(a)

relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i^{\prime}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i^{\prime}}}
,

(b)

only for
h>1
: relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{j}^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{j}}
,

(c)

relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
,

(d)

only for
h>1
: relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime}_{j}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{j}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}
,

(e)

relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y_{i}}
,

(f)

only for
h>1
: relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{j}^{\prime}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime}_{j}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j^{\prime}}}
.

4.

For each
i=1..l
, each
j=1..h
, and each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
, add:

(a)

labelled formulas
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}_{i}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
whenever
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y_{i}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
,

(b)

only for
h>1
: labelled formulas
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime}_{j}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}^{\prime\prime}_{j}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
whenever
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x_{j}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
,

(c)

labelled formulas
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
whenever
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
.

Fig. 5:
Layer lifting as defined in Constructions
7.8
and
7.9

This construction lifts a layer
L
of
\mathfrak{G}
w.r.t. a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in L
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is a singleton cluster, this is a simple lifting as one might expect. Otherwise, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is in a non-singleton cluster, this cluster is duplicated and the lifting of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is put in between the two copies, as indicated in Fig.
5
(ignore label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
for the moment).

In Construction
7.8
, Points 1)–2) ensure that in
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
, the new layer is indeed above
L
as intended and (
\mathord{\leq}\mathsf{tr}
), (
\mathord{\leq}\mathsf{rf}
), and (
R\mathsf{rf}
) are satisfied; Point 3) ensures (
R\mathsf{tr}
), (
\mathsf{F_{1}}
), and (
\mathsf{F_{2}}
); Point 4) ensures
(\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet})
. Thus,
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
is tree-clustered, tree-layered, and structurally saturated. However, it may not be stable because
L
is now an inner layer. It is almost happy due to the saturation of
\mathfrak{G}
but may contain unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}^{\circ}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}^{\circ}
formulas.

Construction 7.9

(Layer Lifting II)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a saturated sequent with
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}^{\circ}
being unhappy
for some label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and some formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
of shape

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}
. We reuse the notation from Construction
7.8
(note that
L
must be a topmost layer of
\mathfrak{G}
due to it being saturated).
We define
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
as follows:

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}
, then
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}}

is
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
to which we add
labelled
formulas
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and call
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
a
suricata label of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}

•

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box B}
, then
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box B}}
is
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
to which we add
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
for a fresh label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
, and additionally add relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\in{\hat{L}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
is in
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
. Here
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
is called a
suricata label of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
.

Fig. 6:
Left: Structure of an unhappy R-triangle loop defined in Def.
7.16
Middle and Right: Structure of an unhappy U-triangle loop defined in Def.
7.17

Informally speaking, sequent
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
contains precisely the relational atoms and labeled formulas that need to be added to
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
so that (i) the unhappy
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}^{\circ}
becomes happy (the suricata label contains the white formula responsible for this happiness) and (ii) the result is still structurally saturated (and tree-layered and cluster-layered). Figure
5
shows the case of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
with the additional fresh label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
(that has no past).
In order to also preserve the property of being stable, we need to add such a layer for all unhappy
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}^{\circ}
in the same layer
L
.

Construction 7.10

(Layer Lifting III)
.

Let
L
be a topmost layer in a saturated non-axiomatic sequent
\mathfrak{G}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{1}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, …,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{m}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{m}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
(of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}
) are all the unhappy formulas in
L
. Then

\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{L}\!\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\!\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{1}}}\!+\!\cdots\!+\!\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{m}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{m}}}
where we assume the sets of fresh labels introduced in each of
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{1}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{1}}},\ldots,\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{m}}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F_{m}}}
to be pairwise disjoint.

If we do this addition of layers naively, we will not terminate. Therefore, we need a loop-check, which could be viewed as a generalized version of a loop-check in sequent calculi for intuitionistic logic.

Definition 7.11

(Simulation of layers)
.

Let
L^{\prime}
and
L
be layers in a layered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
.
A
layer simulation
between
L^{\prime}
and
L
is a non-empty binary relation
\mathsf{S}\subseteq(L^{\prime}\times L)\;\cap\sim
such that for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\in L^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in L
,

(
\mathsf{S1}
)

whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\in L^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, and

(
\mathsf{S2}
)

whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\in L^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.

We say
L^{\prime}

simulates

L
iff there is a layer simulation between
L^{\prime}
and
L
.

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
L^{\prime}
and
L
be layers in a layered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
. If
\mathsf{S}
is a layer simulation between
L^{\prime}
and
L
then, for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in L
, there is a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\in L^{\prime}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
.

Definition 7.13
.

Let
L
be a topmost layer in a layered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
. We say
L

is simulated
iff there is a layer
L^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{G}
, such that
L^{\prime}<L
and
L^{\prime}
simulates
L
.

Definition 7.14

(Lifting Saturation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a saturated non-axiomatic sequent and
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
be all topmost layers in
\mathfrak{G}
that are not simulated.
We define the
lifting saturation
of
\mathfrak{G}
as

\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{L_{1}}+\cdots+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{L_{n}}

where the sets of fresh labels introduced in each of
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{L_{1}},\ldots,\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{L_{n}}
are pairwise disjoint.

Lemma 7.15

(restate = LiftingSaturationStable, name = )
.

If
\mathfrak{G}
is saturated, then
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}
is stable.

7-D

Loop saturation

We have presented all the steps that make the sequent
grow, by adding new labelled formulas or relational atoms.
As a result, unhappy formulas become happy, or the sequent becomes structurally saturated.
Definition
7.14
exhibits a condition under which we stop
applying the growing steps:
namely, when a topmost layer is simulated by a layer below.

However, observe that, in general, new layers are larger than previous ones and there are two sources for the growth. First, in the case of an unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
, a fresh label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
is added, and second, if label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
is in a non-singleton cluster, this cluster is duplicated. Both effects can be seen in Fig.
5
.

For this reason, we need to find a way to
shrink
a layer. This will be done by creating clusters similar to how Def.
7.5
does for
\mathord{\Diamond}^{\bullet}
. The difference is that this time the potential repetition will involve several layers rather than being local to a single layer. The difficulty is that some part of a layer
L_{1}
will be repeated in a layer
L_{2}

occurring above it,
but in order to keep the sequent tree-layered, we cannot create clusters across several layers. The solution we implement here is to create a cluster inside layer
L_{2}
for a part that would be repeated in a future layer, provided that we can repeat in layer
L_{2}
what happened in layer
L_{1}
.
We call such loops
triangle loops
. There are two kinds of such loops. The first kind occurs if the cluster to be created in
L_{2}
is in a part of
L_{2}
that has no past in
L_{1}
. This could be caused for example by repetitions of
\mathord{\Box}^{\circ}
. We call these loops
R-triange loops
. The second kind occurs when the repetition is caused by a repeated duplication of clusters in the layer lifting (see Fig.
5
). We call these loops
U-triangle loops
.

Before we give the formal definitions, observe that all new layers that are created in our algorithm are of the shape
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
, as defined in Construction
7.9
, and each such layer contains exactly one suricata label. This can, therefore, be called
the suricata label of the layer
, and it is (immediately after the lifting) the only label that contains a white formula.
4
4

4

On the other hand, observe that a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
can have several suricata labels in other layers if there is more than one
\mathbin{\supset}^{\circ}
- or
\mathord{\Box}^{\circ}
-formula in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
.

0.

Given a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
, define
\mathfrak{G}_{0}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F})
to be the sequent
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
and let
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{G}_{0}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F})\}
.

1.

For set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{\prime}
, calculate a full saturation
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
.

2.

If all sequents in
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
are axiomatic, then terminate.

The formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
is provable and we can give a proof of
\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
(see Sect.
9
).

3.

Otherwise, pick a non-axiomatic sequent
\mathfrak{G}_{i}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
and compute its lifting saturation
\mathfrak{G}_{i}\mathord{\uparrow}
(see Def.
7.14
).

4.

If
\mathfrak{G}_{i}\mathord{\uparrow}=\mathfrak{G}_{i}
, then terminate.

The formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
is not provable, and sequent
\mathfrak{G}_{i}
defines a countermodel (see Sect.
8
).

5.

Otherwise, set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i+1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigl(\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}\setminus\{\mathfrak{G}_{i}\}\bigr)\cup\{\mathfrak{G}_{i}\mathord{\uparrow}\}
.

6.

Go to Step 1).

Fig. 7:
Proof search algorithm

Definition 7.16

(R-triangle loop)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a saturated sequent with two layers
L_{1}<L_{2}
. We say that clusters
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\subseteq L_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\subseteq L_{2}
form an
R-triangle loop
iff

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
;

2.

there is a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{1}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}
such that there is a layer
L^{\prime}
with
L_{1}<L^{\prime}\leq L_{2}
that contains a suricata label of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{1}}
;

3.

there is a cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
, and
no label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\in L_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
has a past in
L_{1}
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{\not\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
for any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\in L_{1}
.

This R-triangle loop is
unhappy
iff additionally

4.

L_{2}
is a topmost layer;

5.

there are labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}\in L_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
;

6.

there is no suricata label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
;

7.

we do
not
have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
.

(Note that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
may be in a common cluster.)

Figure
6
(Left) illustrates this definition.

Definition 7.17

(U-triangle loop)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a saturated sequent with two layers
L_{1}<L_{2}
.
We say that clusters
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\subseteq L_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\subseteq L_{2}
form a
U-triangle loop
iff

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
;

2.

there is a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{1}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}
such that there is a layer
L^{\prime}
with
L_{1}<L^{\prime}\leq L_{2}
that contains a suricata label of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{1}}
;

3.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}}
.

The U-triangle loop is
unhappy
iff additionally

4.

L_{2}
is a topmost layer;

5.

there are labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}\in L_{2}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
, and either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
;

6.

there is no suricata label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
;

7.

we do
not
have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
.

(Again,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
may be in the same cluster.)

The two possibilities envisioned by this definition, depending on whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
, are illustrated in Fig.
6
(Middle and Right).

Informally, we speak of a
triangle loop
when we can reproduce the steps that started with the creation of
L^{\prime}
and led to
L_{2}
. The loop is unhappy if we can observe some repetition in the new part of
L_{2}
. If this is the case, we can collapse this repetition by creating a cluster, or shrinking an existing cluster, as follows:

Definition 7.18

(Loop saturation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
be saturated sequents. We write
\mathfrak{G}\mathbin{\rightsquigarrow_{\circlearrowright}}\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
iff there is an unhappy R-triangle or U-triangle loop in
\mathfrak{G}
where the labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
are as in Def.
7.16
or Def.
7.17
respectively and
\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{G}
by substituting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}
for all occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}
and closing the resulting
R_{\mathfrak{G}}
under transitivity.
For sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
of sequents, we write
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
iff
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is saturated,
\mathfrak{G}\mathbin{\rightsquigarrow_{\circlearrowright}}\mathfrak{G^{\prime}}
for some
\mathfrak{G}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
, and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S^{\prime}}=\bigl(\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\setminus\{\mathfrak{G}\}\bigr)\cup\{\mathfrak{G}^{\prime}\}
.
We write
{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}^{\ast}_{\circlearrowright}}
for the transitive and reflexive closure of
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}
.
If
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}^{\ast}_{\circlearrowright}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is in normal form w.r.t.
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}
, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is a
loop saturation
of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.

As before, this term is justified because a loop saturation of a saturated set of sequents is saturated.

Lemma 7.19

(restate = LoopSaturationStable, name = )
.

It holds that:

1.

If
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
is saturated and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
is saturated.

2.

The rewrite relation
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}
is terminating.

Definition 7.20

(Full saturation)
.

Let
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
be a set of stable sequents. Let
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
be a semi-saturation of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
, and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
be a saturation of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}
, and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be a loop saturation of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime}
. Then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a
full saturation
of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.

Corollary 7.21

(restate = FullSaturationStable, name = )
.

If a set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
of sequents is stable and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is its full saturation, then
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is saturated.

7-E

Search algorithm

We have now all the ingredients for our proof/countermodel search algorithm, which is presented in Fig.
7
. It produces a sequence
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{0},\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{1},\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{2},\ldots
of sets of saturated sequents using the transformations discussed in this section. We terminate at step
i
if either all sequents in
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
are axiomatic (in that case we can produce a proof in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
, see Sect.
9
) or there is a sequent
\mathfrak{G}_{i}\in\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
that does not grow anymore (in that case we can construct a countermodel, see Sect.
8
).

Fig. 8:
Formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
. The set
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Diamond A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\}
is in all nodes, except
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}
.

Example 7.22
.

Figure
8
contains a (partial) diagrammatic representation of one sequent generated by the algorithm, when run on the valid
\mathsf{IS4}
formula from Example
4.2
. At layer
L_{1}
clusters
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}\}
and
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\}
are generated by saturation (Option
1
of Def.
7.5
). Layer
L_{2}
is created by lifting saturation (Def.
7.14
), where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
is a suricata label and both clusters
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\}
and
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}16}\}
come from
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\}
. The sequent is axiomatic because of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}
.

8
Countermodel Construction

Assume we initiate the algorithm with a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
. If we terminate at Step 4, we have found a saturated sequent
\mathfrak{G}_{i}
such that
\mathfrak{G_{\mathit{i}}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!r}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}^{\circ}
and
\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}_{i}=\mathfrak{G}_{i}
. This means that each topmost layer
of
\mathfrak{G}_{i}
is either happy or simulated.
Thus, for each unhappy layer
L
there is some happy inner layer
L^{\prime}
that simulates
L
via a simulation
\mathsf{S}_{L}
. We now define
\mathfrak{G}_{i}^{\star}
to be obtained from
\mathfrak{G}_{i}
by adding a relational atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathsf{S}_{L}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for some unhappy layer
L
, and by closing the result under transitivity of
\leq
.
This makes all unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
in the
topmost layers
happy and preserves structural saturation. Hence,
\mathfrak{G}_{i}^{\star}
is a happy sequent, which allows to apply Theorem
5.8
to obtain a finite countermodel for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
.

Theorem 8.1

(restate = countermodel, name = )
.

If the algorithm shown in Fig.
7
terminates in Step 4, then
formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
is not a theorem of
\mathsf{IS4}
.

Example 8.2
.

Figure
9
represents (a
\leq
-branch of) a sequent, generated by the algorithm when run on formula (
3
) from Sect.
3
, that is on
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(A\and B)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot})\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
.
If we include label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}
(but remove atom
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}9}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
), the figure depicts the moment when the algorithm finds an unhappy R-triangle loop: employing the terminology from Def.
7.16
, we take
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}
. Then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}
is replaced with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
, and after the loop saturation is no longer present in the sequent.
Then the search on the depicted
\leq
-branch stops because the topmost layer
L_{6}
can be simulated by layer
L_{4}
. By adding relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}}
to the sequent (Theorem
8.1
), we obtain (a part of) the countermodel for our formula.

Fig. 9:
Formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(A\and B)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot})\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
.
