is in all nodes but
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}0}
.

9
Sequent Proof Unfolding

Let us now turn to the case when the algorithm terminates in Step 2. Then all sequents in
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
are axiomatic, and we want to construct a proof of
\Longrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
. For this we are going to simulate the steps of the algorithm by applying the inference rules of
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
, starting with rules
R\mathsf{rf}
and
\mathord{\leq}\mathsf{rf}
to obtain the sequent
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\Longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}r}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
that is the input of the algorithm. On the one hand, this unfolding seems easy because our search algorithm was designed as organized proof search in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
and most steps can indeed be executed by applying the rules of
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
.
However, the difficulties come from
the fact that sequents produced by
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
do not have non-singleton clusters. We call such sequents
proper
.

Definition 9.1

(Vertical sequent)
.

A layered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
vertical
iff for all labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v^{\prime}}
in
\mathfrak{G}
, (a) if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v^{\prime}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v^{\prime}}
, and (b) if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u^{\prime}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u^{\prime}}
, i.e., for each label there is at most one future per layer and at most one past per layer.

Definition 9.2

(Proper layer/sequent)
.

A layer
L
in a tree-clustered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is called
proper
iff all clusters
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\subseteq L
are singletons. A tree-clustered sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is
proper
iff it is tree-layered and vertical and all its layers are proper.

The basic idea of constructing our proof is to mimic the algorithm with a derivation that works “layer by layer.”
The key observation is that whenever we create a cluster in the algorithm, we can
unfold
it, i.e., repeat this cluster arbitrarily often in an actual proof with only proper sequents. And this property is preserved when we lift the cluster to the next layer.

Definition 9.3

(Unfolding)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
a proper sequent
\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}
is called an
n
-unfolding
of a stable sequent
\mathfrak{G}
iff there is a binary
n
-unfolding relation

\mathsf{U}\subseteq\ell(\mathfrak{G})\times\ell(\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}})
that has the following properties, where we assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}}
and write
L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
to denote the layer of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
:

1.

formula invariance
:

{{}_{\mathstrut{}^{\mathstrut}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\sim{{}_{\mathstrut{}^{\mathstrut}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}}
;

2.

R
-invariance
:
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
are not in the same cluster in
\mathfrak{G}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}}
;

3.

layer invariance
:

L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
iff
L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}}\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}}}L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}}}
;

4.

“no-past” invariance
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
has no past in
\mathfrak{G}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}
has no past in
\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}
;

5.

singleton-cluster invariance
:
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\}
is a singleton cluster in
\mathfrak{G}
,
then there is a unique
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}}\in\ell(\mathfrak{\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}})
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}}
;

6.

non-singleton-cluster unfolding
:
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
is a cluster in
\mathfrak{G}
with
\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right|=k\geq 2
,
then for all
i=1..k
and
j=1..n
there are
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z_{i}}\in\ell(\mathfrak{\mathfrak{G}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{i,j}}\in\ell(\mathfrak{\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}})

such that
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z_{i}}\mathsf{U}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{i,j}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z_{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z_{k}}\}
and additionally

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{1,1}}R\cdots R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{k,1}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{1,2}}R\cdots R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{k,2}}R\cdots R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{1,n}}R\cdots R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{z}_{k,n}}
in
\mathfrak{{\hat{G}}}
;

7.

injectivity
:
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{x}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\hat{y}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
.

Note that every
(n+1)
-unfolding
\mathsf{U}
is also an
n
-unfolding and that any proper stable sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is an
n
-unfolding of itself for any
n
, via the diagonal relation
\{({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in\ell(\mathfrak{G})\}
.

To ensure that unfolding only operates with proper sequents, we will now introduce
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
, a proof system whose rules correspond to applications of several
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
steps, thus forcing the rules of
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
to be applied in a certain way.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be a sequent
\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
occurring in
\Delta
. Then we write
{(\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta)}\mathord{\Uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
for
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{G}\mathord{\uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}}
. This allows us to define the following inference rules:

{(\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B})}\mathord{\Uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}}

\scriptstyle\mathbin{\supset}^{\circ}_{\star}\;

\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\mathbin{\supset}B}

{(\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A})}\mathord{\Uparrow}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}}

\scriptstyle\mathord{\Box}^{\circ}_{\star}\;

\mathcal{R},\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}A}

For a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
, we define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\overleftarrow{x}}
to be the set of labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x^{\prime}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
. For a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
of labels, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
for the set
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}\}
of relational atoms. With this notation, we can define the rule:

\mathcal{R},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\overleftarrow{x}}}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}R{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}},\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}\Longrightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\mathord{\Diamond}^{\bullet}_{\star}\;

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
fresh

\mathcal{R},\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A}\Longrightarrow\Delta

Now define
system
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
to be

•

inference rules
\mathsf{id}
,
\bot^{\bullet}
and
\mathord{\Diamond}^{\circ}
as in Fig.
2
;

•

rule
\mathord{\Box}^{\bullet}_{\star}
obtained by setting
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
in
\mathord{\Box}^{\bullet}
from Fig.
2
;

•

rules
\and^{\bullet}_{\star}
,
\and^{\circ}_{\star}
,
\mathbin{\vee}^{\bullet}_{\star}
,
\mathbin{\vee}^{\circ}_{\star}
, and
\mathbin{\supset}^{\bullet}_{\star}
that are variants of
\and^{\bullet}
,
\and^{\circ}
,
\mathbin{\vee}^{\bullet}
,
\mathbin{\vee}^{\circ}
, and
\mathbin{\supset}^{\bullet}
from Fig.
2
respectively, where the principal formula is not deleted in the premises, the premises are closed under monotonicity, and, additionally,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
for
\mathbin{\supset}^{\bullet}_{\star}
;

•

rules
\mathsf{4}^{\bullet}
and
\mathsf{4}^{\circ}
from Fig.
3
;

•

rules
\mathbin{\supset}^{\circ}_{\star}
,
\mathord{\Box}^{\circ}_{\star}
, and
\mathord{\Diamond}^{\bullet}_{\star}
as shown above.

Note that there are no structural rules in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
.

Proposition 9.4

(restate = daggeradm, name = )
.

Every rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
is derivable in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\prime}
, and if
\mathfrak{G}
is the conclusion of an instance of
\mathsf{r}
, then each premise has the form
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{H}
for some
\mathfrak{H}
.

When writing a premise
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{H}
, we assume that
\mathfrak{G}
and
\mathfrak{H}
are disjoint, i.e., all labelled formulas and relational atoms of
\mathfrak{H}

are
absent from the conclusion.

Definition 9.5

(Touch)
.

Let
\mathsf{r}
be an instance of a rule in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
with a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
in its conclusion.
We say that
\mathsf{r}

touches
a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in\ell(\mathfrak{G})
iff for some premise
\mathfrak{G}+\mathfrak{H}
of
\mathsf{r}
, either we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{H}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{H}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
for some label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
or we have
\mathfrak{H},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
or
\mathfrak{H},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
for some formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
. We say that
\mathsf{r}

touches a set
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
of labels
iff
\mathsf{r}
touches some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X}
.

Definition 9.6

(Restricted sequent/forbidden label)
.

A sequent
\mathfrak{G}
is called
restricted
iff it is equipped with a set
\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{G})\subseteq\ell(\mathfrak{G})
of
forbidden
labels satisfying two conditions:

1.

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{G})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{G})
, i.e., all parents of a forbidden label are forbidden;

2.

if
L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}<_{\mathfrak{G}}L_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\in\ell(\mathfrak{G})
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{G})
, i.e., all labels from the inner layers are forbidden.

All labels in
\ell(\mathfrak{G})
that are not forbidden are
allowed
.

In the following, we assume that all sequents are restricted, in particular, all sequents occurring in derivations.

Definition 9.7

(Tidy rule/derivation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{G}
be the conclusion of an instance of a rule
\mathsf{r}
in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
. We say that
\mathsf{r}
is
tidy
iff
(i)
\mathsf{r}
does not touch any forbidden label in
\mathfrak{G}
, and
(ii) the forbidden labels for each premise
\mathfrak{K}
of
\mathsf{r}
are

\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{K})=\begin{cases}\ell(\mathfrak{G})&\text{if }\mathsf{r}\in\{\mathbin{\supset}^{\circ}_{\star},\mathord{\Box}^{\circ}_{\star},\mathord{\Diamond}^{\bullet}_{\star}\},\\
\mathcal{f}(\mathfrak{G})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(4)

A derivation
\mathcal{D}
is
tidy
iff it consists of tidy rule instances.

In other words, rules in tidy derivations do not touch forbidden labels and either keep all labels and forbidden labels unchanged or introduce new allowed labels turning all old labels into forbidden. This is sufficient, in particular, to ensure that all sequents in a tidy derivation are proper.

Proposition 9.8

(restate = properrestrict, name = )
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a tidy derivation. If the conclusion of
\mathcal{D}
is proper, then every sequent in
\mathcal{D}
is also proper.

Definition 9.9

(Unfoldable)
.

A derivation
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is called an
n
-unfolding of a stable set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
iff each premise of
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is an
n
-unfolding of some sequent from
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.
For a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
, a stable set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}

is
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
-unfoldable
iff for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there is
a tidy derivation
\mathcal{D}_{n}
of
\mathfrak{G}_{0}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F})
(as defined in Step 0 of the algorithm in Fig.
7
) such that
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is an
n
-unfolding of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}
.

Clearly,
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{\prime}=\{\mathfrak{G}_{0}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F})\}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
-unfoldable. It now has to be shown that all operations performed in the algorithm, in particular, the rewrite relations
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
,
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
, and
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}
, preserve this propery. For
\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\mathsf{s}}
, this is straightforward, as we simply apply the inference rules of
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
to match the semi-saturation steps.
For
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\Diamond}}
and
\mathbin{\mathbin{\lower 1.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{$\rightsquigarrow$\hss}\raise 1.07639pt\hbox{$\rightsquigarrow$}}_{\circlearrowright}}
, we need to unfold the newly created clusters. The basic idea is to simply repeat all proof steps that lead to the first occurrence of the cluster
n
-times.

Lemma 9.10

(restate = UnfoldingLemma, name = Unfolding Lemma)
.

All sets
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
of sequents generated by the algorithm from Fig.
7
are
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
-unfoldable.

Hence, when we terminate in Step 2, we can construct a
1
-unfolding of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
, which constitutes a proof in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}^{\star}
because an unfolding of an axiomatic sequent is always axiomatic.

Theorem 9.11

(restate = Provable, name = )
.

If the algorithm shown in Fig.
7
terminates in Step 2, then the formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
is a theorem of
\mathsf{IS4}
.

10
Termination

We have already established in Sect.
7
that every
step in our algorithm (shown in Fig.
7
)
terminates. It remains to show that we cannot run through the main loop
forever, i.e., sequence
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{0},\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{1},\ldots,\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i},\ldots
eventually terminates either in Step 2 (we find a proof) or
in Step 4 (we find a countermodel). The basic idea is to restrict the
size of a layer in the sequents of
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
. Then the number of distinct possible layers is finite,
hence, we will eventually find a simulation (Defs.
7.11
,
7.13
).

For the remainder of this section, we assume that a formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
in Step 0 has
n
subformula occurrences.

We define the
size

\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\right|
of a label
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
in a sequent
\mathfrak{G}
to be the number of distinct formula occurrences
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
in
\mathfrak{G}
.

Lemma 10.1

(restate = sizelabel, name = )
.

The size of a label occurring in a sequent of some
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
is at most
n
. And there are
2^{n}
many equivalence classes of labels with respect to
\sim
.

The
size

\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}\right|
of a cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
is the number of labels in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C}
.

Lemma 10.2

(restate = sizecluster, name = )
.

The size of a cluster occurring in a sequent of some
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
is at most
2^{n}
. And there are
2^{2^{n}}-1
many equivalence classes of (non-empty) clusters with respect to
\sim
.

The algorithm visits only stable sequents. A layer
L
in such a sequent is a tree of clusters. Let
M
be a branch in
L
, i.e., a sequence
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{l}}
of clusters from the root to a leaf.
The
length

\left|M\right|
of
M
is the sum
\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}\right|+\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}\right|+\cdots+\left|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{l}}\right|
.

Lemma 10.3

(restate = lengthbranch, name = )
.

The length of a branch in a layer in a sequent in a set
\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\hbox to0.0pt{$\mathfrak{S}$\hss}\mkern 0.75mu\mathfrak{S}_{i}
is bounded, and the bound is determined by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}F}
.

Theorem 10.4

(restate = TerminationAlg, name = Termination)
.

The proof search algorithm given in Fig.
7
is terminating.

11
Conclusion

In this paper we have solved a problem open for almost 30 years. Our solution has two key ingredients.

First, the use of the fully labelled system with relational atoms for both binary relations enabled us to give a proof system that has only invertible rules and also gives a closer correspondence between sequents and models.

Second, although the identification of labels during during proof search to realize loops is
a priori
unsound, however, under the right circumstances, we can preserve soundness if we organize the proof search in a certain systematic way.

We conjecture that the same method can also be applied to
\mathsf{IK4}
, which is
\mathsf{IS4}
without the
\mathsf{t}
-axiom and which is the other logic in the intuitionistic version of the
\mathsf{S5}
-cube for which decidability is an open problem. In fact, the overall argument is the same, but in many definitions and proof arguments, there would be subtle differences due to the absence of reflexivity. For this reason a full treatment of
\mathsf{IK4}
would go beyond the scope of this paper.

The implementation of the algorithm is object of current work.
In a future work we would like to investigate the complexity of provability in
\mathsf{IS4}
.

Acknowledgements.
Roman Kuznets is supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) project ByzDEL (P 33600).
Marianna Girlando is supported by the Horizon 2021 programme, under the
Marie Skłodowska-Curie grant CYDER (101064105).

-A

Examples

This section contains some examples illustrating how the search algorithm (Fig.
7
) works. Refer to Example
5.2
for an explanation of the diagrammatic notation.

For reasons of space, we represent only one sequent of those generated by the algorithm, that is, either an axiomatic sequent or a sequent from which a countermodel for the formula at the root can be generated. Recall that even if an axiomatic sequent is found the algorithm might continue running: for it to stop (at step 2), all sequents in the set need to be axiomatic. Conversely, the algorithm stops (at step 4) if it finds a sequent containing enough information to build a countermodel.

Example .1

(Valid formula)
.

Let us consider formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, which is valid in
\mathsf{IS4}
.
Figure
10
represents one sequent generated by the algorithm. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
be the set
\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Diamond A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\}
. To each label in the figure (except
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}
) we associate
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, plus the formulas explicitly displayed next to the node in the figure.
The following
\leq
-relations are not displayed but are present in the sequent:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}10}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}11}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}16}}
.

At layer
L_{1}
the search on both
R
-branches stops in virtue of option
1
of Def.
7.5
(saturation): there,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}5}
is replaced with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}
. This generates two non-singleton clusters:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\}
.
Then, since all labels in
L_{1}
are almost happy, lifting saturation is applied: the rewrite “lifts” one copy of the layer for each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathbin{\supset}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
-formula
present in the sequent. We only represent one of such layers,
L_{2}
, generated from formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}
belongs to the cluster
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}
, the cluster is duplicated in
L_{2}
, following Defs.
7.8
–
7.9
.
Then, after a semi-saturation step, we have that both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}}
. Thus we stop the search along this branch. If all the sequents manipulated by the algorithm eventually result in axiomatic sequents, then the algorithm stops and, according to Theorem
9.11
, the formula at the root is a theorem of
\mathsf{IS4}
. Indeed, its derivation in
\mathsf{lab}\mathsf{IS4}_{\leq}
can be found in Fig.
4
(Left).

Fig. 10:
Formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box(\mathord{\Diamond}A\and\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(c\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\Diamond}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
.

Fig. 11:
Formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(A\and B)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot})\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
. A loop saturation step is applied to the leftmost sequent, and the result of the saturation is the rightmost sequent.

Example .2

(Non-valid formula, R-triangle loop)
.

We now consider formula
3
from Sect.
3
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(A\and B)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot})\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
.
On the left of Fig.
11
we represent one sequent generated by the algorithm.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(A\and B)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A\and B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}\}
. To each label in the figure (except
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}0}
), we associate
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}
, plus the formulas explicitly displayed next to the node.
To be precise, the figure displays only one
\leq
-branch of the sequent: formulas
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
are in all the labels, and every time they are unhappy they are also lifted, generating a new layer (which is not displayed).

At layer
L_{6}
an unhappy R-triangle loop is detected: employing the terminology from Def.
7.16
, we take
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{1}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p}_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{2}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}C_{r}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}s}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}t}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}
.
On the right of Fig.
11
is represented the sequent resulting from the loop saturation, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}
is replaced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}
.
Observe that the cluster
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}\}
remains unchanged.
After the loop saturation, proof search along the
\leq
-branch depicted stops: this is because layer
L_{6^{\prime}}
can be simulated by layer
L_{4}
. By adding relational atoms
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\mathord{\leq}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}}
to the sequent (Theorem
8.1
), which are the dashed arrows pointing downwards in the figure, we obtain (a part of) the countermodel for our formula.
To complete the countermodel, we need to take into account the layers generated from unhappy
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Box^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathbin{\supset}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}
formulas present in the sequent. Specifically,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}}
are unhappy. These formulas give rise to more
\leq
-branches in the sequent (but all branches are finite, and there is only a finite number of them).

Fig. 12:
Formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}D\and\mathord{\Diamond}c)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}D}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
. A loop saturation step is applied to the topmost sequent, and the result of the saturation is represented in the middle sequent. Then, a loop saturation is also applied to the middle sequent, and the result is the lowermost sequent.

Example .3

(Non-valid formula, U-triangle loop)
.

Let us consider formula
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}D\and\mathord{\Diamond}c)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}D}={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}(a\mathbin{\supset}b)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}}
. Figure
12
illustrates one sequent generated by the search algorithm. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}D\and\mathord{\Diamond}c)^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}D\and\mathord{\Diamond}c^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}D^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}c^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\}
.
To each label we associate the following sets, color-coded in the figure:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}(\mathord{\Diamond}D\and\mathord{\Diamond}c)\mathbin{\supset}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}\}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}\}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}10}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}19}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}29}

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}8}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}6}\sim

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}D^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a\mathbin{\supset}b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\circ}}}\}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}18}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}15}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}\sim

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}28}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}23}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}21}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}9}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}7}\sim

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\Gamma^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}c^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bullet}}}\}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}17}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}14}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}13}\sim

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}27}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}24}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}22}\sim

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}16}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}26}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}25}

As in the previous example, only one

-branch is represented.

happy. The labels originated by lifting this branch are present in all layers.

As in Example

, in layer

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}4}\}

At

-
L_{2}
,
L_{3^{\prime}}
, and continues (middle sequent in Fig.

).

Then, after a lifting saturation, layer

Proofs from Section

\completeness

*

Proof.

The worlds
W
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}}
are the labels of the sequent. Conditions
\mathsf{F_{1}}
and
\mathsf{F_{2}}
, the transitivity and reflexivity of
\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}
and
\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}
, and the monotonicity of
V
all follow by construction due to structural saturation. Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}}
is a transitive and reflexive birelational model. It only remains to show the two properties about forcing, that we prove by mutual induction on the size of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
, proceeding by case analysis on the main connective of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}A}
:

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\bullet}
: it is not possible for a happy sequent.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}^{\circ}
: we have
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\bot}}
by definition.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
: by Definition
5.7
,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\circ}
: it is not the case that
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}^{\bullet}
by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, hence,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}a}
by Definition
5.7
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\and C}^{\bullet}
: by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, both
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
. Then
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
by IH. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\and C}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\and C}^{\circ}
: by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, either
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\circ}
. Then either
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
by IH. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\and C}
.

•

Cases for
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\vee}C}^{\bullet}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\vee}C}^{\circ}
are analogous.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\supset}C}^{\bullet}
: consider any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
.
By (
\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet}
)-structural saturation,
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\supset}C}^{\bullet}
.
By happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, either
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
or
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\bullet}
. By IH, either
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
or
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
. Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
implies
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\supset}C}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\supset}C}^{\circ}
: by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, there is a world
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
,
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
, and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}^{\circ}
. By IH,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}C}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B\mathbin{\supset}C}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}B}^{\bullet}
: by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, there is a world
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\bullet}
. By IH, we have
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
and, therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}B}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}B}^{\circ}
:
by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, we have
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}^{\circ}
for all worlds
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
. Thus, by IH,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
whenever
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\not\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Diamond}B}
.

•

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}^{\bullet}
:
consider arbitrary
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
.
By (
\mathsf{mon}^{\bullet}
)-structural saturation,
\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!y}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}^{\bullet}
. By happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}

. Thus, by IH,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}B}
whenever

and

. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{G}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\Vdash{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}

\mathfrak{G},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\!x}\mathord{:}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.6,0.1}\mathord{\Box}B}^{\circ}
: by happiness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
, there exist
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and

Proofs from Section
6

We start with a proposition that is not in the main text but simplifies many arguments.

Proposition .4
.

Structurally saturated sequents
\mathfrak{G}
are also saturated w.r.t.
R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}
:

({\mathsf{F}})

if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
, then there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
.

Proof.

There must be a sequence
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{0}},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}
of labels with
n\geq 0
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{0}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, and for each
0\leq i\leq n-1
, either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i+1}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i+1}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{i}}
. We use induction on
n
. If
n=0
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}
suffices. Otherwise, by IH, there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n-1}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n-1}}
, there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
by
(\mathsf{F_{1}})
-structural saturation such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n-1}}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}
, there is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
by
(\mathsf{F_{2}})
-structural saturation such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x_{n}}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}v}\mathord{\mathbin{R_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
. Either way,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\mathord{R^{\leftrightarrow}_{\mathfrak{G}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}u}
.
∎

\OrderLayers

*

Proof.

For reflexivity, consider any layer
L
. For any label

)-structural saturation,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}}
. Hence,
L\leq L
by Definition
6.2
.

For transitivity, let
L_{1}\leq L_{2}
and
L_{2}\leq L_{3}
for layers
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
, and
L_{3}
. By Definition

,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y^{\prime}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}y}\mathord{\mathbin{\leq_{\mathfrak{G}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}}
for some labels
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}\in L_{1}
,

, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}z}\in L_{3}

)-structural saturation (Proposition

), there is a label
